People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, nice to meet you, Doc. I guess... well, what brings me in is that ever since I stopped working—I retired a little bit ago, you know, and I’ve worked hard my whole life, pretty much right out of high school, always on my feet, always managing things—but since I stopped, I just... I can’t seem to slow down. 

You'd think I'd want to just sit in my armchair and watch the game or finally take it easy, but it’s like my body didn't get the memo that we're off the clock. I wake up and I’m just up and moving, bouncing from one little job around the house to the next, all day long. I'll be fixing a loose hinge on the kitchen cabinet, and then before I know it I'm out sweeping the garage, and then I'm pulling weeds by the driveway... just constantly going like I still have a whole work crew waiting on me to give them their orders for the day. I mean, I'm 65 years old, I should be enjoying the quiet, but I'm just pacing around finding these little projects from sunup to sundown, and I just can't seem to turn that motor off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's funny you ask that, because my wife, she actually asked me almost the exact same thing the other day. I was out in the garage at six in the morning reorganizing all my socket wrenches by size, and she comes out with her coffee and asks if I'm worried about something, you know, if something is eating at me. And I really had to stop and think about it for a minute. Because I don't feel worried at all. I'm not sitting around chewing my fingernails or sweating over the electric bill or anything like that. I mean, the house is paid off, the kids are grown and doing fine—my oldest is out in Ohio and the youngest is down in Florida—so there's really nothing for me to be anxious about right now. 

And on the flip side, I'm not feeling on top of the world, either. I wouldn't say I'm elated or unusually upbeat, like you said. I'm not jumping for joy. I'm just... well, I'm just doing things. It’s just this hum in my chest. Like yesterday, I wasn't particularly happy or sad when I decided to power wash the back deck, I just looked at it and thought that it needed doing, and then I spent four hours out there spraying it down. So, no, my mood is just fine, pretty level, honestly. I'm just regular old me, not overly happy or nervous. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's a good question, Doc. I mean, looking back, I was a handyman for a long, long time. And in that line of work, you're always busy, right? You're always moving from one job site to the next, fixing this, patching that, measuring, cutting, hammering. You gotta stay on top of it, or things fall apart. So, I guess I was always a guy who liked to keep his hands busy. But it was different back then. It was work. It was directed, you know? I had a list, I checked things off, and at the end of the day, I went home and I could actually sit down and have a beer on the porch. 

But this... this feeling of just having to go, go, go, all the time, where I can't even sit through a half-hour television program without getting up to fiddle with the thermostat or check the locks on the doors... that really, truly started being a real issue, a real problem for me, after I retired. I hung up my tool belt, officially, about a year and two months ago. Yeah, it was right around the end of October, a year and two months back. We had a little retirement party, my wife made a cake, the whole nine yards. And I thought, "Great, time to relax." But almost right away, that's when this constant motor just kicked in and wouldn't shut off. So, yeah, I was always a hard worker, but this... this level of just not being able to stop, that's definitely only been since I retired fourteen months ago.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's funny you mention the physical stuff, because I've always been pretty in tune with my body, working with my hands and my back all those years. You learn to know when you pulled a muscle or when you're just stiff from the cold. But as far as tension... no, not really. I don't feel like my muscles are knotted up or anything like that. I mean, my shoulders aren't up around my ears, and I don't feel that tight, coiled-spring kind of feeling that guys get when they're stressed out. 

And sleep... well, sleep has always been pretty straightforward for me. I hit the pillow, and I'm out. Even now, with all this pacing around during the day, by the time night rolls around, I'm just tired from all the walking and the fixing and the sweeping. I don't lay there staring at the ceiling, tossing and turning, you know, with my mind racing about what I need to do tomorrow. I just go to sleep. 

And as for feeling on edge... I wouldn't say on edge. On edge sounds like you're waiting for the other shoe to drop, like you're expecting bad news or something to go wrong. And I don't feel like that at all. I don't jump when the phone rings or get startled easily. It's really just this feeling of needing to be doing something, moving my feet, using my hands. It's not a nervous energy, it's just... energy. Like I said, it's like a motor running, but it's not a motor that's vibrating or rattling the whole car, it's just idling a little too high. So, no, I wouldn't say I have any of those physical tension things you're talking about, just the constant moving around.

I don't know if that makes sense, but it's like my mood is just sitting right there in neutral while the rest of my body is stuck in overdrive.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back over your whole life—before you retired—would you say you've always had this strong drive to stay busy and keep things in order, or is this level of constant activity something new that started only after you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's a good question, Doc. I mean, looking back, I was a handyman for a long, long time. And in that line of work, you're always busy, right? You're always moving from one job site to the next, fixing this, patching that, measuring, cutting, hammering. You gotta stay on top of it, or things fall apart. So, I guess I was always a guy who liked to keep his hands busy. But it was different back then. It was work. It was directed, you know? I had a list, I checked things off, and at the end of the day, I went home and I could actually sit down and have a beer on the porch. \n\nBut this... this feeling of just having to go, go, go, all the time, where I can't even sit through a half-hour television program without getting up to fiddle with the thermostat or check the locks on the doors... that really, truly started being a real issue, a real problem for me, after I retired. You learn to know when you pulled a muscle or when you're just stiff from the cold. But as far as tension... no, not really. I don't feel like my muscles are knotted up or anything like that. I mean, my shoulders aren't up around my ears, and I don't feel that tight, coiled-spring kind of feeling that guys get when they're stressed out. \n\nAnd sleep... well, sleep has always been pretty straightforward for me. I hit the pillow, and I'm out. Even now, with all this pacing around during the day, by the time night rolls around, I'm just tired from all the walking and the fixing and the sweeping. I wouldn't say on edge. On edge sounds like you're waiting for the other shoe to drop, like you're expecting bad news or something to go wrong. And I don't feel like that at all. So, no, I wouldn't say I have any of those physical tension things you're talking about, just the constant moving around.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf your wife asked you to stop what you're doing and just sit down for an hour instead of finishing a task, how would that make you feel—would you feel very anxious or upset, or just a bit restless?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"Sitting through a quiet meeting or church talk, I feel like I’ve got to shift around, tap my foot, or get up and stretch even when I know I should stay put.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if she asked me to just sit down for an hour... boy, that would be tough. I wouldn't say I'd be anxious, like, I wouldn't be scared or upset with her, but just restless. Really, really restless. It's like... well, let me give you an example. We go to church every Sunday, right? Have for forty years. Same pew, third row from the back on the left side. Pastor Miller, he's a good man, gives a solid sermon, usually about twenty, twenty-five minutes long. But lately, sitting through that... it's like torture. Not because of what he's saying, but because I just cannot stay still. I'm sitting there, and I know I'm supposed to be quiet and respectful, but I feel like I've just got to shift my weight, or cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then cross the other one. 

I start tapping my foot, you know, just a little bit at first, but then my wife will put her hand on my knee and give me that look. You know the look. So I stop tapping the foot, but then my hands start going. I'm fiddling with the hymn book, tracing the letters on the cover, or I'm looking at my watch, thinking about how much time is left. I just get this overwhelming urge to get up and stretch my legs, to just walk to the back of the sanctuary and stand there. And I know I shouldn't, I know I need to stay put, but it takes every ounce of willpower I have just to stay in that seat. 

So if my wife asked me to just sit in the living room for an hour and do nothing while I was in the middle of fixing the hinge on the screen door? I'd probably sit down for about three minutes, and then I'd start noticing a scuff mark on the baseboard, or a cobweb in the corner of the ceiling, and before I knew it, I'd be up and grabbing a rag or the broom. I just can't sit there and be idle. It's not a nervous thing, it's just this physical need to be moving, shifting, doing something. Sitting still is just about the hardest work I could do right now.
=====================================

I had a list, I checked things off, and at the end of the day, I went home and I could actually sit down and have a beer on the porch. \n\nBut this... this feeling of just having to go, go, go, all the time, where I can't even sit through a half-hour television program without getting up to fiddle with the thermostat or check the locks on the doors... that really, truly started being a real issue, a real problem for me, after I retired. I hung up my tool belt, officially, about a year and two months ago. Yeah, it was right around the end of October, a year and two months back. We had a little retirement party, my wife made a cake, the whole nine yards. And I thought, \"Great, time to relax.\" But almost right away, that's when this constant motor just kicked in and wouldn't shut off. So, yeah, I was always a hard worker, but this... this level of just not being able to stop, that's definitely only been since I retired fourteen months ago.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess whether he experiences the cognitive/physical worry and tension symptoms required for GAD (e.g., muscle tension, edginess, sleep disturbance from worry) versus just goal-directed restlessness tied to retirement, to differentiate GAD from an adjustment reaction.\", \"question\": \"Even though you don't feel anxious about specific things like money or family, do you notice physical tension—like tight muscles, feeling on edge, or trouble falling asleep—that seems separate\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you mention the physical stuff, because I've always been pretty in tune with my body, working with my hands and my back all those years. You learn to know when you pulled a muscle or when you're just stiff from the cold. But as far as tension... no, not really. I don't feel like my muscles are knotted up or anything like that. I mean, my shoulders aren't up around my ears, and I don't feel that tight, coiled-spring kind of feeling that guys get when they're stressed out. \n\nAnd sleep... well, sleep has always been pretty straightforward for me. I hit the pillow, and I'm out. Even now, with all this pacing around during the day, by the time night rolls around, I'm just tired from all the walking and the fixing and the sweeping. I don't lay there staring at the ceiling, tossing and turning, you know, with my mind racing about what I need to do tomorrow. I just go to sleep. \n\nAnd as for feeling on edge... I wouldn't say on edge. On edge sounds like you're waiting for the other shoe to drop, like you're expecting bad news or something to go wrong. And I don't feel like that at all. I don't jump when the phone rings or get startled easily. It's really just this feeling of needing to be doing something, moving my feet, using my hands. It's not a nervous energy, it's just... energy. Like I said, it's like a motor running, but it's not a motor that's vibrating or rattling the whole car, it's just idling a little too high. So, no, I wouldn't say I have any of those physical tension things you're talking about, just the constant moving around.\n\nDoctor: If your wife asked you to stop what you're doing and just sit down for an hour instead of finishing a task, how would that make you feel—would you feel very anxious or upset, or just a bit restless?\n\nPatient: Well, if she asked me to just sit down for an hour... boy, that would be tough. I wouldn't say I'd be anxious, like, I wouldn't be scared or upset with her, but just restless. Really, really restless. It's like... well, let me give you an example. We go to church every Sunday, right? Have for forty years. Same pew, third row from the back on the left side. Pastor Miller, he's a good man, gives a solid sermon, usually about twenty, twenty-five minutes long. But lately, sitting through that... it's like torture. Not because of what he's saying, but because I just cannot stay still. I'm sitting there, and I know I'm supposed to be quiet and respectful, but I feel like I've just got to shift my weight, or cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then cross the other one. \n\nI start tapping my foot, you know, just a little bit at first, but then my wife will put her hand on my knee and give me that look. You know the look. So I stop tapping the foot, but then my hands start going. I'm fiddling with the hymn book, tracing the letters on the cover, or I'm looking at my watch, thinking about how much time is left. I just get this overwhelming urge to get up and stretch my legs, to just walk to the back of the sanctuary and stand there. And I know I shouldn't, I know I need to stay put, but it takes every ounce of willpower I have just to stay in that seat. \n\nSo if my wife asked me to just sit in the living room for an hour and do nothing while I was in the middle of fixing the hinge on the screen door? I'd probably sit down for about three minutes, and then I'd start noticing a scuff mark on the baseboard, or a cobweb in the corner of the ceiling, and before I knew it, I'd be up and grabbing a rag or the broom. I just can't sit there and be idle. It's not a nervous thing, it's just this physical need to be moving, shifting, doing something. Sitting still is just about the hardest work I could do right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from those moments in church, would you say this constant need to keep moving has caused any real strain in your marriage or friendships, or kept you from doing things you'd otherwise enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, strain... strain might be a strong word, but I suppose it has caused some friction, yeah. I mean, my wife and I, we've been married forty-two years, so she knows me inside and out, but lately... well, she's really had to step in and manage things for me in a way she never used to. She’s actually had to completely take over keeping the calendar. We've got this big calendar hanging on the side of the fridge—you know, one of those ones they give out at the bank with the different covered bridges for every month? Anyway, I used to be pretty good at keeping track of my own schedule, my own jobs, my own errands. But now, because I'm always up and moving, starting this, fixing that, getting distracted by a squeaky floorboard when I was supposed to be heading out the door to the hardware store... she has to write every single thing down for me. 

And not just that, she’s constantly checking behind me. I’ll tell her I fixed the latch on the side gate, and she actually has to go out into the yard and make sure I finished it, because half the time she finds I just left my tools sitting there in the grass because I saw a weed that needed pulling, or I noticed the gutter was dripping, and I just wandered off to do that instead. It frustrates her, I know it does, having to mother me like that at my age. I hate that she feels like she has to supervise me.

And as for friends, or doing things I enjoy... well, it’s definitely gotten in the way there, too, and it's been a little embarrassing, if I'm being honest. I’ve always been pretty involved locally, you know? But lately, I’ve had to apologize to the community center and the garden group more than once for letting people down. Like, just a couple of weeks ago, I told the garden group I'd come down on a Tuesday morning and help them build some new raised beds for the spring—they're trying to get a head start on the tomatoes and the squash and whatnot. I had the wood all loaded up in the truck and everything. But I got so busy puttering around the garage that morning, reorganizing my C-clamps, sweeping out the sawdust, just feeling that absolute need to keep my hands moving right then and there... I completely missed the time I was supposed to be there. Didn't even look at the clock until they called my house wondering where I was. 

I felt just terrible. You really hate

Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. I had a list, I checked things off, and at the end of the day, I went home and I could actually sit down and have a beer on the porch. \n\nBut this... this feeling of just having to go, go, go, all the time, where I can't even sit through a half-hour television program without getting up to fiddle with the thermostat or check the locks on the doors... that really, truly started being a real issue, a real problem for me, after I retired. I hung up my tool belt, officially, about a year and two months ago. Yeah, it was right around the end of October, a year and two months back. We had a little retirement party, my wife made a cake, the whole nine yards. And I thought, \"Great, time to relax.\" But almost right away, that's when this constant motor just kicked in and wouldn't shut off. So, yeah, I was always a hard worker, but this... this level of just not being able to stop, that's definitely only been since I retired fourteen months ago.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, you know, it's funny you mention the physical stuff, because I've always been pretty in tune with my body, working with my hands and my back all those years. You learn to know when you pulled a muscle or when you're just stiff from the cold. But as far as tension... no, not really. I don't feel like my muscles are knotted up or anything like that. I mean, my shoulders aren't up around my ears, and I don't feel that tight, coiled-spring kind of feeling that guys get when they're stressed out. \n\nAnd sleep... well, sleep has always been pretty straightforward for me. I hit the pillow, and I'm out. Even now, with all this pacing around during the day, by the time night rolls around, I'm just tired from all the walking and the fixing and the sweeping. I don't lay there staring at the ceiling, tossing and turning, you know, with my mind racing about what I need to do tomorrow. I just go to sleep. \n\nAnd as for feeling on edge... I wouldn't say on edge. On edge sounds like you're waiting for the other shoe to drop, like you're expecting bad news or something to go wrong. And I don't feel like that at all. I don't jump when the phone rings or get startled easily. It's really just this feeling of needing to be doing something, moving my feet, using my hands. It's not a nervous energy, it's just... energy. Like I said, it's like a motor running, but it's not a motor that's vibrating or rattling the whole car, it's just idling a little too high. So, no, I wouldn't say I have any of those physical tension things you're talking about, just the constant moving around.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, if she asked me to just sit down for an hour... boy, that would be tough. I wouldn't say I'd be anxious, like, I wouldn't be scared or upset with her, but just restless. Really, really restless. It's like... well, let me give you an example. We go to church every Sunday, right? Have for forty years. Same pew, third row from the back on the left side. Pastor Miller, he's a good man, gives a solid sermon, usually about twenty, twenty-five minutes long. But lately, sitting through that... it's like torture. Not because of what he's saying, but because I just cannot stay still. I'm sitting there, and I know I'm supposed to be quiet and respectful, but I feel like I've just got to shift my weight, or cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then cross the other one. \n\nI start tapping my foot, you know, just a little bit at first, but then my wife will put her hand on my knee and give me that look. You know the look. So I stop tapping the foot, but then my hands start going. I'm fiddling with the hymn book, tracing the letters on the cover, or I'm looking at my watch, thinking about how much time is left. I just get this overwhelming urge to get up and stretch my legs, to just walk to the back of the sanctuary and stand there. And I know I shouldn't, I know I need to stay put, but it takes every ounce of willpower I have just to stay in that seat. \n\nSo if my wife asked me to just sit in the living room for an hour and do nothing while I was in the middle of fixing the hinge on the screen door? I'd probably sit down for about three minutes, and then I'd start noticing a scuff mark on the baseboard, or a cobweb in the corner of the ceiling, and before I knew it, I'd be up and grabbing a rag or the broom. I just can't sit there and be idle. It's not a nervous thing, it's just this physical need to be moving, shifting, doing something. Sitting still is just about the hardest work I could do right now.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
